\part{Algebraic-parameter rigidity of the omnific integers}\label{opa:part:par}

This part is manuscript 02. Its question is different from that of
\cref{opa:part:aut}: not which automorphisms preserve a support condition, but
whether $\Oz$ admits nonconstant \emph{algebraic} families of embeddings. It does
not: the decisive property is that every nonzero element divides an element with
roots of every order. It does admit formal families of all orders. The relation to
\cref{opa:part:aut} is recorded in \cref{opa:par:sec:relation}.

\section{Conventions and the root-covered property}\label{opa:par:sec:foundations}

\subsection{Conventions of this part}\label{opa:par:sub:conv}

Ring homomorphisms are unital, domains are commutative integral domains, and
$\N=\{0,1,2,\dots\}$; degrees of polynomials are ordinary integers. This part keeps
its source's \emph{large-monomial} convention, which is that of the other omnific
reports: for a characteristic-zero field $k$, a unital subring $\oo\subseteq k$ and a
nonzero divisible ordered abelian group $\Gamma$,
\[
 k((X^\Gamma))=\Bigl\{\sum_{g\in S}a_gX^g:S\subseteq\Gamma\text{ reverse well ordered}\Bigr\},
 \qquad X^g=t^{-g},
\]
so the leading exponent $\ell(x)=\max\supp x$ is the largest, and
$\ell(xy)=\ell(x)+\ell(y)$. The ideal $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$ consists of the series supported
in $\Gamma^{>0}$ (the same set as $\Pi$ in \cref{opa:part:aut}), and
$\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)=\oo+\Pi_k(\Gamma)$ is the ring $\Ro$ of \cref{opa:part:aut},
the Diophantine report's $\mathcal R_\oo(\Bbbk,\Gamma)$. For surreals $X=\omega$, so
$\Oz=\mathcal R_\Z(\R,\No)$ and $\Og=\mathcal R_{\Z[i]}(\C,\No)$. Renamings from the
source: its $R_\Gamma(D,k)$ and $\mathcal P_\Gamma(k)$ are
$\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$ and $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$; its principal convex subgroup $H(a)$ is
$C(a)$, the transcendence report's notation ($C_\delta$ in \cref{opa:part:aut}; the
quotient report's $H_a$ means the opposite, the elements infinitesimal relative to
$a$); its covering element $c$ is
$z$; its bounded-scale field $K_b$ is $K_{\mathrm{bd}}$ and its stages $H_j$ are
$\Gamma_{\le j}$. Elsewhere in this part the letters $R,B,A,K,F,L$ denote rings and
fields local to each statement.

The general theorems are proved for sets. Their conclusions for $\Oz$, $\Og$, $\No$
and $\SC$ use class functions and set-supported normal forms. A statement about one
polynomial contains finitely many coefficients and variables, so the degree
arguments apply to a class-sized coefficient domain unchanged. No proper-class
scheme $\operatorname{Spec}(\Oz)$ and no class compactness or Noetherian theorem is
used; the finite-type theorem is a theorem about sets, applied to the set-sized
models of \cref{opa:par:sec:workspaces}. Expressions involving $\Aut(\Oz)$ or
$\ML$ of a class ring abbreviate statements about each automorphism or derivation;
$(b,x)\mapsto D_b(x)$ is one definable class operation. For cancellation against a
class-sized $B$, the transcendence-degree arguments involve only finite tuples of
indeterminates inside one rational-function field \src{02}.

\begin{definition}[\src{02}]\label{opa:par:def:rootcover}
A domain $R$ is \emph{root-covered} if for every $0\ne a\in R$ there are $b\in R$ and
$z=ab\ne0$ such that for every $n\ge1$ some $z_n\in R$ has $z_n^n=z$. (No
compatibility between the $z_n$ is required; the terminology is local to this
report.)
\end{definition}

Fields are root-covered. The interesting examples have few units: $\Oz^\times=\{\pm1\}$
and $\Og^\times=\{\pm1,\pm i\}$, and in general $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)^\times=\oo^\times$ by
additivity of leading exponents (the Diophantine report's \lbl{odg:prop:units},
\lbl{odg:def:lem:units}).

\begin{lemma}[Localization \src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:localize}
If $R$ is root-covered and $S\subseteq R\setminus\{0\}$ is multiplicative, then $S^{-1}R$ is
root-covered.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$(a/s)(sb)=z$, and the roots $z_n$ remain.
\end{proof}

Root-covered is sufficient for the conclusions below and is not asserted
necessary; failure of the monomial criterion is not used to claim failure of
cancellation or stable rigidity \src{02}.

\section{The monomial-divisibility criterion}\label{opa:par:sec:hahn}

For $a>0$ in $\Gamma$ let $C(a)=\{g:|g|\le na\text{ for some }n\ge1\}$; $a$ is an
\emph{order unit} if $C(a)=\Gamma$.

\begin{theorem}[Monomial divisibility \src{02}; after \lbl{bst:thm:fieldcriterion}]\label{opa:par:thm:scale}
For $\Gamma\ne0$ divisible and $R=\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$ the following are equivalent:
\textup{(i)} $\Gamma$ has no order unit; \textup{(ii)} for every $0\ne x\in R$ some
monomial $X^c$ with $c>0$ is divisible by $x$ in $R$. Then the quotient can be chosen
in $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$, and $R$ is root-covered.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Assume (i). The support of $x$ lies in $[0,a]$ for some $a>0$. The inverse of $x$ in
the Hahn field has support in the subgroup generated by $\supp x$, hence in $C(a)$
(write $x=\alpha X^h(1+\varepsilon)$ and expand the geometric series). Choose $c>na$ for all
$n$; then $X^cx^{-1}$ has strictly positive support, lies in $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$, and
$x\cdot X^cx^{-1}=X^c$. Divisibility of $\Gamma$ gives $(X^{c/n})^n=X^c$. Conversely,
if $a$ is an order unit and $x=1+X^a$, the unique Hahn quotient
$X^c/(1+X^a)=\sum_{n\ge1}(-1)^{n-1}X^{c-na}$ has a nonzero coefficient at a negative
exponent, so it is not in $R$.
\end{proof}

This is the transcendence report's field criterion \lbl{bst:thm:fieldcriterion}
(\fn{docs/surreal/transcendence-over-bounded-support}: the bounded-support ring is a
field if and only if $\Gamma$ has no order unit, by the same two arguments, the
inverse support in a proper convex subgroup and the geometric series of $1-t^u$),
restricted to positive-support elements; its ``order unit'' is defined identically.
Source 02's audit read only the openings of the omnific reports and did not see
it. The positive-support phrasing is what the rigidity theorems use.

\begin{example}[\src{02}; \cite{LM}]\label{opa:par:ex:omega}
In $\No$, $\omega>n$ for all ordinary $n$, so
$\omega^\omega/(1+\omega)=\omega^{\omega-1}-\omega^{\omega-2}+\omega^{\omega-3}-\cdots$ is an
omnific integer: $1+\omega$ divides $\omega^\omega$. With exponent group $\Q$ no exponent
can absorb the tail. The example is L'Innocente and Mantova's; the distinction is
a larger Archimedean scale, not convergence.
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[Omnific root coverage \src{02}]\label{opa:par:cor:ozcover}
$\Oz$ and $\Og$ are root-covered: every nonzero element divides a positive real
monomial $\omega^c$, with quotient of strictly positive support.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
This is immediate from the global monomial clearing \lbl{odg:thm:fractions} of the
Diophantine report, applied to $x^{-1}$: some $\omega^c$ makes $\omega^cx^{-1}$ purely
infinite. Source 02 gives the same argument through \cref{opa:par:thm:scale}, using
a strict upper bound of the set $\{na:n\ge1\}$; the coefficients' order is not used.
\end{proof}

\section{Finite-type domains carry no varying embedding}\label{opa:par:sec:finitetype}

The results of this section concern set-sized fields and domains.

\begin{lemma}[Unbounded roots \src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:rootsfield}
Let $F/K$ be finitely generated and $u\in F^\times$. If $u$ has $n$th roots in $F$ for
an unbounded set of $n$, then $u$ is algebraic over $K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Otherwise extend $u$ to a transcendence basis $u,t_2,\dots,t_r$, with
$[F:E]=d<\infty$ for $E=K(u,t_2,\dots,t_r)$. $Z^n-u$ is Eisenstein at the prime $u$ of
$K(t_2,\dots,t_r)[u]$, hence irreducible over $E$ by Gauss's lemma, so an $n$th root
has degree $n\le d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Algebraic constants \src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:constants}
If $F/K$ is finitely generated, the relative algebraic closure $K'$ of $K$ in $F$ is
finite over $K$; if $A$ is an integral $K$-algebra with $\Frac A=F$, then
$C_A=A\cap K'$ is a field finite over $K$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
With a transcendence basis $\mathbf t$ and $[F:K(\mathbf t)]=d$, every finitely generated
$L\subseteq K'$ has $[L:K]=[L(\mathbf t):K(\mathbf t)]\le d$ (adjoining algebraically
independent indeterminates preserves linear independence; $\mathbf t$ stays
independent over $L$). An $L$ of maximal degree contains every element of $K'$. For
$0\ne a\in C_A$ the minimal polynomial has nonzero constant term, which expresses
$a^{-1}$ as a polynomial in $a$ over $K$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finitely many unit translates \src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:translates}
Let $A$ be a finitely generated integral $K$-algebra and $f\in A$ transcendental over
$K$. Only finitely many $\lambda\in K$ make $f-\lambda$ a unit of $A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Extend $f$ to a transcendence basis $f,t_2,\dots,t_r$ of $\Frac A$, and put
$B_0=K[f,t_2,\dots,t_r]$. Generators of $A$ are algebraic over $\Frac B_0$; clearing
denominators gives $0\ne d\in B_0$ with $A$ integral over $B=B_0[1/d]$ in the common
overring. If $(f-\lambda)^{-1}\in A$, it lies in $\Frac B_0$ and is integral over the
integrally closed $B$, so $f-\lambda$ divides a power of $d$ in the UFD $B_0$, hence
divides $d$; the primes $f-\lambda$ are pairwise nonassociate. The argument does not
assume $B\subseteq A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Finite-type embedding rigidity \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:finitetype}
Let $R$ be a characteristic-zero root-covered domain, $K$ a characteristic-zero
field, and $A$ a finitely generated integral $K$-algebra. Every injective
homomorphism $\phi:R\to A$ has image in the field
$C_A=\{x\in A:x\text{ algebraic over }K\}$, finite over $K$, and extends uniquely to
$\Frac R\to C_A$. If $K$ is relatively algebraically closed in $\Frac A$, then $C_A=K$.
Neither $K$-linearity of $\phi$ nor a map $K\to R$ is assumed.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $a\ne0$ take $ab=z$ and $z_n^n=z$. Then $\phi(z)\ne0$ has roots of all orders, so it
is algebraic (\cref{opa:par:lem:rootsfield}) and a unit of $A$
(\cref{opa:par:lem:constants}); hence $\phi(a)$ is a unit. If $f=\phi(a)$ were
transcendental, every $f-m=\phi(a-m)$, $m\in\Z$, would be a unit (as $a-m\ne0$),
contradicting \cref{opa:par:lem:translates}. So $\phi(R)\subseteq C_A$, and nonzero
images are invertible there.
\end{proof}

The theorem is nontrivial only for set-sized $R$, such as the Hahn models of
\cref{opa:par:sec:workspaces}; it is not a disguised statement that a proper class
cannot inject into a set. One must not replace $C_A$ by the whole relative algebraic
closure: $A=\Q[x,\sqrt2x]$ has $\Frac A=\Q(\sqrt2)(x)$ but $C_A=\Q$, since elements of
$A$ have rational constant term; no normality of $A$ is assumed \src{02}. If $K$ is
relatively algebraically closed in $\Frac A$, every embedding $R\to A$ has image in
$K$, and none exists if $\Frac R$ does not embed in $K$.

\begin{theorem}[Pointed parameter spaces \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:pointed}
Let $R$ be a set-sized characteristic-zero root-covered domain, $F=\Frac R$, $A$ a
finite-type integral $F$-algebra with an $F$-algebra map $\varepsilon:A\to F$. If a
ring homomorphism $\psi:R\to A$ has $\varepsilon\psi$ equal to the inclusion, then
$\psi(x)=x$ for all $x$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\psi$ is injective, so $\psi(R)\subseteq C_A$. The restriction of $\varepsilon$ to the
field $C_A$ is injective and fixes $F$; for $a\in C_A$, $a-\varepsilon(a)\in C_A$ has
image $0$, so $C_A=F$.
\end{proof}

Examples are $A=F[s_1,\dots,s_r,1/h]$ with an $F$-point where $h\ne0$; relations,
singularities and localizations are allowed, and relative algebraic closedness is not
required. The hypothesis is one common finite-type algebra containing the images of
all of $R$, not separate formulas in unrelated algebras. For a finite extension
$L/F$, $L[s]$ contains the root-covered $L$ as constants and has no $F$-valued
augmentation when $L\ne F$ \src{02}.

\section{Polynomial and Laurent coefficient rigidity}\label{opa:par:sec:coefficient}

These proofs work over arbitrary coefficient domains, need no finite generation and
assume no preservation of infinite sums \src{02}.

\begin{theorem}[Polynomial coefficient rigidity \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:poly}
If $R$ is root-covered and $B$ a domain, every injective $\phi:R\to B[T_1,\dots,T_n]$
has image in $B$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\deg\phi(z)=n\deg\phi(z_n)$ for all $n$ forces $\deg\phi(z)=0$, and
$\deg\phi(a)+\deg\phi(b)=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Laurent units \src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:laurentunits}
For a domain $B$, the units of $B[U_1^{\pm1},\dots,U_q^{\pm1}]$ are the
$b\mathbf U^{\mathbf e}$ with $b\in B^\times$ and $\mathbf e\in\Z^q$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The widths $M_j-m_j$ of the $U_j$-exponents are nonnegative and additive, so a unit
has a single exponent vector.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Laurent coefficient rigidity \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:laurent}
If $R$ is root-covered and $B$ a domain, every injective
$\phi:R\to B[\mathbf U^{\pm1}]$ has image in $B$; hence so does every injective
$\phi:R\to B[\mathbf T,\mathbf U^{\pm1}]$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The largest and smallest $U_j$-exponents of $\phi(z)$ are divisible by every $n$, so
$\phi(z)$ is a nonzero constant, a unit over $\Frac B$, and $\phi(a)$ is a Laurent
monomial by \cref{opa:par:lem:laurentunits}. If $a\ne-1$ the same holds for $\phi(a)+1$,
which forces $\phi(a)$ to be constant. For mixed targets apply
\cref{opa:par:thm:poly} over $B[\mathbf U^{\pm1}]$ first.
\end{proof}

Injectivity and a domain target are needed: $x\mapsto x+sD(x)$ into $R[s]/(s^2)$ can
be nonconstant with identity specialization (\cref{opa:par:sec:formal}). No
noninjective analogue for all targets is claimed \src{02}.

\begin{theorem}[Strong coefficient invariance \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:cancellation}
Let $R$ be root-covered, $B$ any commutative unital ring, and
$\Phi:R[X_1,\dots,X_p,U_1^{\pm1},\dots,U_q^{\pm1}]\to
B[Y_1,\dots,Y_{p'},V_1^{\pm1},\dots,V_{q'}^{\pm1}]$ an isomorphism with
$p+q=p'+q'<\infty$. Then $\Phi(R)=B$, $q=q'$ and $p=p'$. In particular, for
$R=\Oz$ or $\Og$, every isomorphism $R[\mathbf X_n]\cong B[\mathbf Y_n]$ carries $R$
onto $B$: $R$ is strongly invariant and cancels against an arbitrary coefficient
ring with the same number of variables. If $B$ is also root-covered, the equality of
total variable counts can be dropped.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The target is a domain, so $B$ is, and \cref{opa:par:thm:laurent} gives
$\Phi(R)\subseteq B$. With $F=\Frac\Phi(R)\subseteq L=\Frac B\subseteq E$,
$\trdeg_FE=p+q=p'+q'=\trdeg_LE$, so $L/F$ is algebraic. $E$ is a rational function
field over $F$, which has no new algebraic constants (a nonconstant rational function
is transcendental, one variable at a time), so $B\subseteq F$, and comparing coefficients
gives $B\subseteq\Phi(R)$. Polynomial variables add no units, and the quotient unit
groups give $\Z^q\cong\Z^{q'}$. If both rings are root-covered, apply the first step
to $\Phi$ and $\Phi^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

Equality of total variable counts cannot be dropped for arbitrary $B$:
$R[X_1,X_2]=B[X_2]$ with $B=R[X_1]$. The general statements do not solve the
unrestricted cancellation problem for finitely generated algebras; the
strong-invariance terminology and history are older \cite{Hirano} \src{02}.

\begin{theorem}[Automorphisms of polynomial and torus rings \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:aut}
For a root-covered $R$, every automorphism of $R[\mathbf T]$ or $R[\mathbf U^{\pm1}]$
preserves $R$; for polynomial extensions, restriction to $R$ is split surjective onto
$\Aut R$, with kernel the $R$-algebra automorphisms. Every automorphism of $R[T]$ is $T\mapsto uT+b$ with $u\in R^\times$,
$b\in R$, over a unique $\sigma\in\Aut R$; every automorphism of
$R[\mathbf U^{\pm1}]$ is $U_i\mapsto u_i\prod_jU_j^{m_{ji}}$ over $\sigma$, with
$u_i\in R^\times$ and $(m_{ji})\in\GL_q(\Z)$. For $\Oz$, $u\in\{\pm1\}$; for $\Og$,
$u\in\{\pm1,\pm i\}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Apply \cref{opa:par:thm:laurent} to an automorphism and its inverse; coefficientwise
extension is the section. In one variable, $\deg g\deg f=1$; units give the torus
formula via \cref{opa:par:lem:laurentunits}.
\end{proof}

The affine and torus formulas are standard once coefficients are preserved; the new
omnific conclusion is that \emph{every} ring automorphism preserves $\Oz$. No
classification of multivariable polynomial automorphisms over $R$ and no claim about
the Jacobian conjecture follows \src{02}.

\section{Locally nilpotent derivations, coactions and gradings}\label{opa:par:sec:lnd}

A derivation $D$ of a domain is \emph{locally nilpotent} if every element is killed by
some iterate, and $\ML(A)=\bigcap_{D\in\LND(A)}\ker D$. Derivations are ring
derivations, hence $\Z$-linear; $A$ need not contain $\Q$.

\begin{lemma}[\src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:lnddegree}
For a locally nilpotent $D$ of a characteristic-zero domain,
$\deg_D(a)=\max\{j:D^ja\ne0\}$ satisfies $\deg_D(ab)=\deg_Da+\deg_Db$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
$D^{r+s}(ab)=\binom{r+s}rD^ra\,D^sb\ne0$, and higher iterates vanish.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Stable locally nilpotent rigidity \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:ML}
Let $R$ be a characteristic-zero root-covered domain. Every locally nilpotent
derivation of any domain containing $R$ kills $R$, and
$\ML(R[T_1,\dots,T_n])=R$ for every finite $n$. In particular
$\ML(\Oz[\mathbf T])=\Oz$, $\ML(\Og[\mathbf T])=\Og$, and $\Oz$ has no nonzero locally
nilpotent derivation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$\deg_D(z)=m\deg_D(z_m)$ for all $m$ forces $\deg_Dz=0$, hence $\deg_Da=0$. The partial
derivatives $\partial/\partial T_j$ are locally nilpotent with common kernel $R$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[No coactions or gradings \src{02}]\label{opa:par:cor:coaction}
If $R$ is root-covered, a homomorphism $\psi:R\to R[s_1,\dots,s_r]$ with identity
specialization at $\mathbf s=0$, or $\theta:R\to R[u_1^{\pm1},\dots,u_r^{\pm1}]$ with
identity specialization at $\mathbf u=1$, is the constant inclusion; so there are no
nontrivial additive-group or split-torus coactions of finite rank, and every
$\Z^r$-grading of $R$ is concentrated in degree zero.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Identity specialization makes the map injective; apply
\cref{opa:par:thm:poly,opa:par:thm:laurent}. A grading gives
$\sum x_{\mathbf m}\mapsto\sum x_{\mathbf m}\mathbf u^{\mathbf m}$.
\end{proof}

A Hahn expansion $\sum a_gX^g$ is not a grading: it has infinitely many terms, and the
no-grading result concerns finite homogeneous decompositions. \Cref{opa:par:thm:ML}
does not say that $R[\mathbf T]$ has no locally nilpotent derivation, and it is not a
solution of a general stable-rigidity conjecture \cite{CD} \src{02}.

\section{Euler derivations and formal flows}\label{opa:par:sec:formal}

The parameter $s$ in this section is a new formal indeterminate, not a surreal and not
a Hahn monomial.

\begin{proposition}[Support-preserving Euler derivations \src{02}]\label{opa:par:prop:Euler}
For additive $\chi:\Gamma\to(k,+)$, $D_\chi(\sum a_gX^g)=\sum\chi(g)a_gX^g$ is a
$k$-linear derivation of $k((X^\Gamma))$ that preserves $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$, kills
$\oo$ and maps $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$ into $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$; and
$D_\chi D_\psi=D_\psi D_\chi$ for additive $\chi,\psi$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The coefficient of $X^h$ in $xy$ is a finite sum of $a_gb_{h-g}$, and
$\chi(h)=\chi(g)+\chi(h-g)$ splits it; $\chi(0)=0$.
\end{proof}

In the notation of \cref{opa:part:aut}, $D_\chi$ is the shift-zero derivation
$t^\gamma\mapsto\chi(-\gamma)t^\gamma$. Division by $n!$ is allowed on $\Pi_k(\Gamma)$, and
$D_\chi^n(x)\in\Pi_k(\Gamma)$ for $n\ge1$, so
\begin{equation}\label{opa:par:eq:formalexp}
 \EE_{\chi,s}(x)=\sum_{n\ge0}\frac{D_\chi^n(x)}{n!}s^n
\end{equation}
has coefficients in $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$; no division of an integer constant occurs.

\begin{theorem}[Compatible formal flows \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:formal}
$\EE_{\chi,s}$ is a unital ring homomorphism $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)\to
\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)[[s]]$ with identity specialization, satisfying
$\EE_{\chi,s}\EE_{\chi,t}=\EE_{\chi,s+t}$; its coefficientwise extension fixing $s$ is
an automorphism of $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)[[s]]$ with inverse from $-s$, and it induces
compatible automorphisms of $\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)[s]/(s^{N+1})$ for every $N$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
$D_\chi^n(xy)/n!=\sum_rD_\chi^r(x)/r!\cdot D_\chi^{n-r}(y)/(n-r)!$, first in the Hahn
field; the coefficient of $s^rt^m$ in both sides of the flow law is
$D_\chi^{r+m}(x)/(r!m!)$; each coefficient uses finitely many inputs. Substituting $t=-s$
is valid since both parameters have positive order.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Nonalgebraization at the first jet \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:nonalg}
If $\Gamma$ has no order unit and $\chi\ne0$, the reduction of $\EE_{\chi,s}$ modulo $s^2$
is nonconstant and is induced by no homomorphism
$\psi:\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)\to\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)[s]$ with
$\ev_{s=0}\psi=\id$, and $D_\chi$ is not locally nilpotent.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Some $g>0$ has $\chi(g)\ne0$; then $D_\chi^n(X^g)=\chi(g)^nX^g\ne0$. By
\cref{opa:par:thm:scale} the ring is root-covered, and by \cref{opa:par:cor:coaction}
every such $\psi$ is constant.
\end{proof}

Formal automorphisms exist to \emph{all} orders; the obstruction appears only when a
single polynomial family must realize even the first-order part. A formal power
series ring is not of finite type, and $R[s]/(s^2)$ is not a domain, so there is no
conflict with \cref{opa:par:thm:finitetype} \src{02}. The flows involve no analytic
convergence, no fine-topology limit and no identification of $s$ with a surreal
infinitesimal, and they are not an exponentiation of a derivation \emph{inside} the
Hahn field \src{02}.

\subsection{The monomial orbit field}

Let $R=\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$ be set sized, $F=\Frac R$ (which contains $k$:
$a=aX^g/X^g$) and $W=\chi(\Gamma)$, a $\Q$-subspace of $k$. In $F((s))$ put
\begin{equation}\label{opa:par:eq:orbit}
 L_\chi=F\Bigl(\frac{\EE_{\chi,s}(X^g)}{X^g}:g>0\Bigr)=F(e^{ws}:w\in W),
 \qquad e^{ws}=\sum_nw^ns^n/n!.
\end{equation}
It uses only transformed monomials; it is not claimed to contain the image of every
Hahn series \src{02}.

\begin{lemma}[Formal exponential independence \src{02}]\label{opa:par:lem:expindep}
For distinct $\lambda_j\in F$ the $e^{\lambda_js}$ are $F$-linearly independent; for
$\Q$-independent $w_1,\dots,w_r$ the $e^{w_js}$ are algebraically independent over $F$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Comparing coefficients of $s^n$, $n<m$, gives a Vandermonde system. A polynomial
relation is a linear relation among $e^{(\alpha\cdot\mathbf w)s}$ with distinct exponents.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Monomial orbit field \src{02}]\label{opa:par:thm:orbitfield}
$\trdeg_FL_\chi=\dim_\Q W$. If $\chi\ne0$, $L_\chi$ lies in no finitely generated
extension of $F$ inside $F((s))$, so $\EE_{\chi,s}:R\to F[[s]]$ factors through no
finite-type $F$-algebra with an $F$-algebra second map.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A $\Q$-basis $(w_j)$ of $W$ gives algebraically independent $e^{w_js}$, and each $e^{ws}$
has a power equal to a monomial in finitely many of them, so $L_\chi$ is algebraic over
$F(e^{w_js})$. For $0\ne w\in W$, $e^{ws}$ is transcendental and has roots $e^{(w/n)s}$ in
$L_\chi$, so \cref{opa:par:lem:rootsfield} excludes a finitely generated overfield, even
when $\dim_\Q W=1$. A factorization would put the image of $R$ in a finite-type domain
$B\subseteq F[[s]]$ with $\Frac B\supseteq L_\chi$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An omnific family of commuting directions}

\begin{theorem}[Commuting omnific derivations \src{02}; after \lbl{osq:prop:classder}]\label{opa:par:thm:Db}
For $b\in\No$, $D_b(\sum a_g\omega^g)=\sum q_b(g)a_g\omega^g$ is an $\R$-linear derivation
of $\No$ and, with complex coefficients, a $\C$-linear derivation of $\SC$ commuting
with conjugation; it preserves $\Oz$ and $\Og$. The $D_b$ commute, and every finite
family with distinct indices is linearly independent over $\Oz$, respectively $\Og$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
\Cref{opa:par:prop:Euler} with $\chi=q_b$. For $m_c=\omega^{\omega^c}\in\Oz$,
$D_b(m_c)=m_c$ if $b=c$ and $0$ otherwise; applying $\sum_ja_jD_{b_j}=0$ to $m_{b_\ell}$
gives $a_\ell m_{b_\ell}=0$ in a domain.
\end{proof}

These derivations, with the test elements $m_c$, are the quotient report's
class-valued derivations \lbl{osq:prop:classder}
(\fn{docs/surreal/set-sized-quotients-of-omnific-integers}), which combine that report's
sources 06, 08 and 10 and state independence over $\R$; the same evaluation gives
independence over the omnific ring, as 02 states, and 02 adds their commutation and
flows. In the $t$-convention of \cref{opa:part:aut}, $D_b(t^\gamma)=-q_b(\gamma)t^\gamma$.

\begin{corollary}[Multi-parameter flows \src{02}]\label{opa:par:cor:multiflow}
For distinct $b_1,\dots,b_r$ and $R=\Oz$ or $\Og$, the map
\[
 x\longmapsto\sum_{\alpha\in\N^r}\frac{D_{b_1}^{\alpha_1}\cdots D_{b_r}^{\alpha_r}(x)}
 {\alpha_1!\cdots\alpha_r!}\,s_1^{\alpha_1}\cdots s_r^{\alpha_r}
 \quad\text{into }R[[s_1,\dots,s_r]]
\]
obeys the additive formal group law; its coordinate directions are independent, and no nonzero
coordinate direction is induced by a polynomial family on $R$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Compose one-parameter flows, which commute; independence is \cref{opa:par:thm:Db}, and
the last claim is \cref{opa:par:thm:nonalg} in one parameter.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[\src{02}]\label{opa:par:ex:flow}
For $b=0$, $q_0(g)$ is the real constant coefficient of the exponent $g$:
$D_0(\omega^r)=r\omega^r$ for real $r$, $D_0(\omega^\omega)=0$, and
$\EE_{0,s}(\omega)=\omega+\omega s+\frac\omega2s^2+\frac\omega6s^3+\cdots$, every coefficient in
$\Oz$ although $\frac12,\frac16\notin\Oz$; ``$\omega e^s$'' abbreviates this series and is
not the surreal exponential. Also
$D_0\bigl(\omega^\omega/(1+\omega)\bigr)=-\omega^{\omega+1}/(1+\omega)^2$, again omnific, supported at
$\omega-n$ for positive ordinary $n$ (checked coefficientwise to 29 terms by the
placement dossier).
\end{example}

\begin{corollary}[A continuum of independent orbit elements \src{02}]\label{opa:par:cor:continuum}
For $R=\Oz$, $F=\No$ (or $R=\Og$, $F=\SC$), the monomial orbit field $F(e^{rs}:r\in\R)$
of $D_0$ has transcendence degree $2^{\aleph_0}$ over $F$, in the sense of a set-sized
transcendence basis with finite algebraic relations.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
$q_0(r)=r$, and $e^{rs}=\EE_{0,s}(\omega^r)/\omega^r$ for $r>0$. A $\Q$-basis of $\R$, made
positive, has $2^{\aleph_0}$ elements; \cref{opa:par:lem:expindep} holds over the class
field $F$ since each relation uses finitely many coefficients.
\end{proof}

Only a lower bound is asserted for the field generated by the formal images of all
Hahn series \src{02}.

\begin{remark}[Actual automorphisms remain abundant \src{02}]\label{opa:par:rem:actual}
For additive $\chi:\No\to\R$ and ordinary $t\in\R$,
$\tau_{\chi,t}(\sum a_g\omega^g)=\sum e^{t\chi(g)}a_g\omega^g$ is a support-preserving
automorphism of $\No$ and $\SC$ preserving $\Oz$ and $\Og$, with
$\tau_{\chi,t}\tau_{\chi,u}=\tau_{\chi,t+u}$ and positive multipliers. These character
twists are established \cite{KKS} and not claimed new (\cref{opa:rem:twists}); they
prevent reading the rigidity theorems as $\Aut(\Oz)=1$. What fails is realization of a
family by a homomorphism into a polynomial or finite-type parameter algebra: with
$\chi=q_0$ the twists send $\omega^r$ to $e^{tr}\omega^r$ for all real $r$ at once, which no
single polynomial parameter algebra can carry.
\end{remark}

\section{Set-sized workspaces and a fraction-field warning}\label{opa:par:sec:workspaces}

Let $\Gamma=\bigoplus_{j\ge0}\Q e_j$, ordered by the sign of the coefficient at the
largest index, so $e_{j+1}>ne_j$ for all $n$: $\Gamma$ is divisible with no order unit.
For $(\oo,k)=(\Z,\R)$ or $(\Z[i],\C)$, $R=\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$ is a root-covered set
(\cref{opa:par:thm:scale}), so all the results of this part apply to it in ordinary set
theory. The order embedding $e_j\mapsto\omega^j$ of exponent groups induces
$X^{e_j}\mapsto\omega^{\omega^j}$ and embeds $R$ in $\Oz$, respectively $\Og$; the coordinate
projections give commuting derivations already here, and replacing $\Q e_j$ by $\R e_j$
gives a set-sized realization of \cref{opa:par:cor:continuum} \src{02}.

Let $\Gamma_{\le j}=\bigoplus_{\ell\le j}\Q e_\ell$ (convex) and
$K_{\mathrm{bd}}=\bigcup_jk((X^{\Gamma_{\le j}}))$.

\begin{proposition}[Bounded-scale fraction field \src{02}; after \lbl{bst:prop:localdensity}]\label{opa:par:prop:fracworkspace}
For this $R$, $\Frac R=K_{\mathrm{bd}}\subsetneq k((X^\Gamma))$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
An element of $R$ with positive leading exponent in $\Gamma_{\le j}$ has all its exponents
in $\Gamma_{\le j}$ by convexity, so quotients lie in $K_{\mathrm{bd}}$. Conversely
$y\in k((X^{\Gamma_{\le j}}))$ gives $X^{e_{j+1}}y\in R$. Finally $\sum_{j\ge0}X^{-e_j}$ is a Hahn
series in no $k((X^{\Gamma_{\le j}}))$.
\end{proof}

This is the transcendence report's localization proposition
\lbl{bst:prop:localdensity} together with its field criterion (condition (2) of
\lbl{bst:thm:fieldcriterion}) for this group. For the full class ring,
$\Frac\Oz=\No$ and $\Frac\Og=\SC$ (\lbl{odg:thm:fractions}); that fact does not justify
replacing $K_{\mathrm{bd}}$ by the full Hahn field, since a fixed set-sized group may lack
an upper bound for a set of its scales even without an order unit \src{02}.

\section{Relation to Part I and the questions of this part}\label{opa:par:sec:relation}

The two parts are complementary. \Cref{opa:part:aut} concerns derivations with
\emph{positive} shifts and their exponentials inside the Hahn field; this part concerns
shift-zero (support-preserving) derivations and needs an external formal parameter.
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt]
\item 02's question on formal directions below is partly answered by
\cref{opa:part:aut}: the positive-shift derivations preserving $\Oz$ are classified
exactly, by $c_\delta(C_\delta)=0$ (\cref{opa:thm:derivation}) and, on common positive
shifts, by $a_\delta(C_\delta)=0$ (\cref{opa:thm:derivcriterion}). Those with a common
well-ordered set of shifts integrate \emph{inside} $\No$ to automorphisms of $\Oz$
(\cref{opa:thm:lie}), so they are integrable by an honest automorphism rather than only
by a formal flow; for an arbitrary contracting strong one this holds granted
\cref{opa:fact:import} for $\Gamma=\No$ (\cref{opa:rem:import}). The shift-zero derivations, the Hasse--Schmidt extensions of other
derivations of $\Oz$, and derivations with no common well-ordered shift set are not
covered.
\item There is no conflict with \cref{opa:par:thm:ML}: the shift derivations
$D_{\delta,\theta}$ restrict to derivations of $\Oz$ but are not locally nilpotent, since
$D_{\delta,\theta}^n(t^\gamma)=\theta(\gamma)^nt^{\gamma+n\delta}\ne0$ when $\theta(\gamma)\ne0$; and the
flows $\Phi_s$ are not polynomial in $s$, since $\Phi_s(t^{-\eta})$ involves every power
of $s$.
\item The character twists of \cref{opa:par:rem:actual} are the factor $M_{\chi,\id}$ of
\cref{opa:thm:factorization}.
\end{itemize}
\Cref{opa:par:thm:aut} concerns $\Aut(\Oz[T])$, not $\Aut(\Oz)$, and does not bear on
\lbl{osq:q:invisible}. The rational self-maps of $\Oz$ studied in the quotient report
are maps of the ring given by rational functions, not ring automorphisms of $\Oz[T]$; the
objects differ. Derivations into set-sized modules vanish (\lbl{osq:thm:derivations}),
which does not conflict with \cref{opa:par:thm:Db}, whose values lie in the class module
$\Pi$.

\begin{question}[Beyond the scale criterion \src{02}]\label{opa:par:q:orderunit}
For a nonzero divisible $\Gamma$ \emph{with} an order unit, which rings
$\mathcal R_\oo(k,\Gamma)$ remain strongly invariant or stably rigid?
\end{question}
\Cref{opa:par:thm:scale} shows that their elements need not divide monomials; it does not
show that cancellation or locally nilpotent rigidity fails.

\begin{question}[Formal directions \src{02}; re-scoped]\label{opa:par:q:directions}
Which derivations of $\Oz$ preserve its purely infinite ideal, and which admit an
iterative integral Hasse--Schmidt extension $\Oz\to\Oz[[s]]$? How much larger is this
class than the Euler family of \cref{opa:par:thm:Db}?
\end{question}
\emph{Status.} Partly answered for positive shifts by \cref{opa:part:aut}, as recorded
above; open otherwise. The factorial argument of \cref{opa:par:thm:formal} is a
sufficient condition for the displayed family and is not asserted to classify all
integrable derivations or all formal automorphisms tangent to the identity.

\begin{question}[Reduced parameter algebras \src{02}]\label{opa:par:q:reduced}
How far does coefficient rigidity extend to reduced parameter rings with several
irreducible components when a prescribed specialization stays injective on the source?
\end{question}
The present proofs apply to domain quotients on which the map stays injective; a
component can kill root-cover elements, and the dual-number example shows that
nilpotent parameter rings need a different statement.

\part{Automatic strongness and proper embeddings of the omnific pair}\label{opa:part:as}

This part is built from three manuscripts of batch 30, numbered 11, 12 and 13 here.
It has two themes. The first is automatic strongness: every ring automorphism of
$\Oz$ extends to a strong automorphism of $\No$, which answers
\cref{opa:q:strong} and extends \cref{opa:thm:factorization,opa:cor:allautos} to
all automorphisms. The second is embeddings: proper self-embeddings of $\No$ can
preserve and reflect $\Oz$ and still move a real number, and the strong
exact-monomial ones are classified by a convex subgroup of the target value group.
Its labels carry the sub-prefix \lbl{opa:as:}.

\section{Sources, results and conventions of this part}\label{opa:as:sec:intro}

\subsection{Three sources and where the merge chose}\label{opa:as:sub:sources}

The three manuscripts were placed together in commit \texttt{21375f8} as items 03,
04 and 05 of batch 30. They are numbered 11, 12 and 13 here, after their file
prefixes:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{11} \emph{Coefficient Gaps and Proper Self-Embeddings of the Surreal
Field: omnific preservation, topological dichotomies, and nonconjugate copies}, pinned
to \texttt{cf56b89}; files prefixed \fn{11-coefficient-gaps-}.
\item \textbf{12} \emph{Automatic Strongness and Proper Embeddings of the Omnific
Integers: constant-term duality, full stabilizers, and the surreal--surcomplex
boundary}, pinned to \texttt{a6c68ac}; files prefixed \fn{12-automatic-strongness-}.
\item \textbf{13} \emph{Automatic Hahn Linearity of Omnific Isomorphisms: residue
duality, full stabilizers, and coefficient-drifting self-embeddings}, pinned to
\texttt{0865f04}; files prefixed \fn{13-omnific-isomorphisms-}.
\end{itemize}
The three pins are commits of one line that forked at \texttt{c6359e4}, the
placement of source 10. None contains \texttt{b3fa9e2}, the batch-28 write of source
10 into this report. So no author saw \cref{opa:sc:sub:supportcut},
\cref{opa:sc:rem:nonstrong} or the paragraph of partial information after
\cref{opa:q:strong}; their comparisons were with the report of batch 26, and they
cite its strong factorization (\cref{opa:thm:factorization}) and its
strongness-free \cref{opa:cor:allautos} accurately.

Sources 12 and 13 prove the same main theorems, in nearly the same order: scalar
detection of summability, automatic strongness of constant-term-covariant
isomorphisms, automatic strongness of $\Aut(\Oz)$, the factorization of the whole
stabilizer, scalar adjoints, a proper-class obstruction, a proper embedding moving a
real, and surcomplex versions. They are printed once, with both sources named. The
merge made these choices:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Base of the automatic-strongness sections: 12.} Its abstract theorem
assumes only a coefficient isomorphism $\alpha$ and derives $F|_k=\alpha$, where 13
assumes $\sigma(k)=\ell$ (\cref{opa:as:thm:ctiso}); it also proves the factorization
for set-sized groups, the Archimedean corollary, the regular-cardinal case and
retractions for arbitrary strong embeddings. 13's genuinely different material is
added with its proofs: the countable binary witness and its finite-row lemma, kept
as a second route to detection (\cref{opa:as:lem:binary,opa:as:thm:detect});
isomorphisms between different, possibly non-divisible groups
(\cref{opa:as:prop:setpairs,opa:as:cor:setpairs}); the coefficient-matrix formula
(\cref{opa:as:prop:matrix}); the example that constant-term preservation does not
give preservation of $\Oz$ (\cref{opa:as:ex:ctnotOz}); the small-integer-part
fraction example (\cref{opa:as:ex:smallfrac}); and the failure of homogeneity
(\cref{opa:as:cor:homogeneity}).
\item \emph{Base of the embedding sections: 11.} Its bottom-gap classification
(\cref{opa:as:thm:classification}) contains the embeddings of 12 and 13 as instances
(\cref{opa:as:thm:explicit}). The three explicit maps are the same construction with
different order isomorphisms $\No\to\No_{>0}$ or $\No\to(1,\infty)$; they are printed
once. 12's image intersection and annihilator and 13's non-homogeneity are kept.
\item \emph{Printed once, not reprinted.} The reconstruction ingredients that all
three reprove (the ideal $\Pi=\bigcap_nn\Oz$, the existential test, the multiplier
ring, its units, $\Frac\Oz=\No$, fixing of $\R$) are cited from the Diophantine
report and \cref{opa:sec:remember}; the convex-support criterion, which 12 and 13
reprove, is \cref{opa:thm:criterion,opa:sc:thm:supportcut}; the Taylor coefficient
automorphism, which 11 reproves, is \lbl{saut:thm:coeffflow} of the surcomplex
automorphism report.
\item \emph{Credit added.} 12 and 13 do not credit \lbl{saut:thm:coeffflow}, which
already moves a real $b$ to $b+t^\delta$ by a strong automorphism of $\No$; 11 does.
The Taylor motion in every embedding below is that theorem's
(\cref{opa:as:sub:taylor}).
\end{itemize}
No mathematical error was found in the three manuscripts. They agree with one
another and with the rest of the report: automorphisms preserving $\Oz$ fix $\R$
(\cref{opa:cor:allautos}), embeddings need not. Two statements in their delivered
audits are wrong and are recorded in \cref{opa:sub:corrections}; neither affects the
mathematics.

\emph{Batch 31.} Source 14, written independently of 11--13, re-derives the
automatic-strongness results of this part; where it does, 14 is added to the source
tag, and its own material and its notation are in \cref{opa:cm:part}.

\subsection{What this part settles}\label{opa:as:sub:settles}

\begin{itemize}[itemsep=2pt]
\item \Cref{opa:q:strong} is \emph{answered} in all its clauses
(\cref{opa:as:thm:main}): every ring automorphism of $\Oz$ extends uniquely to a
strongly $\R$-linear automorphism of $\No$ whose inverse is strong, so preservation of
$\Oz$ forces strongness. There is no nonstrong $\Oz$-preserving automorphism, so the
clause of 09 (characterize the nonstrong automorphisms of $(\No,\Oz)$ that commute
with normal-form sums) is vacuous, and 10's variant (is every $\Oz$-preserving
$1$-automorphism strong?) has the answer yes.
\item \Cref{opa:thm:factorization,opa:cor:allautos} hold for all automorphisms of
$(\No,\Oz)$ and of the full real Hahn pairs with nonzero divisible set-sized exponent
group (\cref{opa:as:thm:factor}). For Archimedean groups every stabilizer element is a
monomial map (\cref{opa:as:cor:arch}), which upgrades \cref{opa:thm:rank} from
$1$-automorphisms to the whole stabilizer.
\item The second clause of \lbl{osq:q:invisible} (how much can an automorphism of
$\Oz$ do inside $\Pi$ while fixing $\ct$?) is answered (\cref{opa:as:cor:invisible});
its first clause is untouched.
\item Scope. The answers concern $(k,\oo)=(\R,\Z)$: $\Oz$ and the full real Hahn pairs.
For $(\C,\Z[i])$ they hold when conjugation or the natural valuation is preserved
(\cref{opa:as:sec:complex}); whether every automorphism of $\Og$ is strong stays open
(\cref{opa:as:q:gaussian}). For a general characteristic-zero $k$ and unital
$\oo\subseteq k$, as in \cref{opa:thm:criterion}, nothing here removes the strongness
hypothesis (\cref{opa:as:q:otherrings}).
\item Embeddings: a strong field embedding of the full real Hahn pairs sending
monomials to monomials and preserving the integer parts is classified by a coefficient
section into the Hahn field on the \emph{bottom gap}; real numbers can move exactly
when the positive part of the value-group image is not coinitial
(\cref{opa:as:thm:classification,opa:as:cor:rigidity}). Whether every embedding of
$(\No,\Oz)$ reflecting $\Oz$ is strong has a negative answer if there is a measurable
cardinal (\cref{opa:as:q:embeddingstrong}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Conventions and notation of this part}\label{opa:as:sub:notation}

All three sources use the small-$t$ convention \eqref{opa:eq:sign} of
\cref{opa:part:aut}, $t^\gamma=\omega^{-\gamma}$, so the purely infinite series have
\emph{negative} $t$-exponents. For a field $k$ and an ordered abelian group $\Gamma$ put
$K_\Gamma=\Hahn k\Gamma$, $\Pi_\Gamma$ for the series supported in $\Gamma^{<0}$ and
$\mm_\Gamma$ for those supported in $\Gamma^{>0}$. For $k=\R$ put
\[
 \mathcal R_\Gamma=\Z\oplus\Pi_\Gamma,\qquad \mathcal A_\Gamma=\R\oplus\Pi_\Gamma,
\]
as in \cref{opa:thm:threshold}; for $\Gamma=\No$ these are $\Oz$ and $\Ak$. For $k=\C$
write $\Pi^\C_\Gamma$, $\mathcal R^\C_\Gamma=\Z[i]\oplus\Pi^\C_\Gamma$ and
$K^\C_\Gamma=\Hahn\C\Gamma$; for $\Gamma=\No$ these are $\Pi_\C$, $\Og$ and $\SC$. The
constant term $\ct(x)=\coef0x$ is the projection $P_0$ on all of $K_\Gamma$; it is
$k$-linear and not multiplicative, since $\ct(t^\gamma)\ct(t^{-\gamma})=0\ne\ct(1)$ for
$\gamma\ne0$. The \emph{constant-term pairing} is
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:pairing}
 \pairing xy=\ct(xy),\qquad \coef\gamma x=\pairing x{t^{-\gamma}} .
\end{equation}
It is symmetric, $k$-bilinear and nondegenerate, and not positive definite: it is not
an inner product, a trace or a residue integral \src{12, 13}.

\emph{Strong maps.} An additive map is \emph{strongly additive} if it sends every Hahn
summable family to a summable family and commutes with its sum; this is one-directional.
A strongly additive map need not be $\R$-linear: the Taylor maps below move real
constants. As in \cref{opa:sub:notation}, a \emph{strong automorphism} is one whose
inverse is also strongly additive; every theorem below states both directions where it
asserts them. For scalar families in $k$ ``summable'' means ``only finitely many nonzero
terms''; this is not convergence in $\R$ \src{13}.

\emph{Exponent maps.} Here $\tau$ is an \emph{ordered additive embedding} of exponent
groups; when it is onto it is the order automorphism $\tau$ of
\eqref{opa:eq:monomialmap}. The monomial map $M_{\chi,\tau}$ of
\eqref{opa:eq:monomialmap} is defined by the same formula for an embedding $\tau$ and
then is a strong field embedding. A field embedding is \emph{exact-monomial} if it sends
every $t^\gamma$ to a monomial $t^{\tau(\gamma)}$ with coefficient one (11 says
``monomial-preserving''). This does \emph{not} mean commuting with the omega-map
$x\mapsto\omega^x$, and it excludes characters and principal-unit corrections \src{11}.

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{longtable}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.67\textwidth}}
\toprule
Notation & Meaning in \cref{opa:part:as}, and the sources' symbols\\
\midrule
\endhead

Sources: $I_\Gamma$, $A_\Gamma$ (11); $A_G$, $B_\R$ (12); $\mathcal J_\Gamma$, $\mathcal A$
(13).\\
$\pairing xy$ & $\ct(xy)$. Sources: $\langle x,y\rangle_0$ (12, 13); 11 writes $[x]_0$ for

Sources: $\ell_g$, $a_\delta$ (12); $L_\gamma$, $a_\delta$ (13).\\

The collisions are resolved in the new material only, and the tempting false readings
are wrong:
\begin{itemize}[itemsep=1pt]
\item 11's $C_\tau$ is \emph{not} a principal convex subgroup $C_\delta$. It is the
largest convex subgroup of the target group meeting $\tau(\Gamma)$ only in $0$; in the
notation of \cref{opa:def:convex}, $\gap_\tau=\bigcap_{h\in\tau(\Gamma)^{>0}}H_h$. For
the explicit maps below it happens to be a principal convex subgroup, $C_1$ or
$C_\omega$ (\cref{opa:as:lem:gaps}). 11's $H=\tau(\Gamma)$ is written $\tau(\Gamma)$,
leaving $H_g$ to \cref{opa:def:convex}; 11's class $\mathcal F$ of finite surreals is
$C_1$.
\item 12's $\theta$ is an \emph{exponent embedding}, not an additive profile as in
$D_{\delta,\theta}$; it is written $\iota_{\mu_{12}}$. 13 uses $\tau$ both for the order
automorphism of $M_{\chi,\tau}$ and for its exponent embedding; the second is
$\iota_{\mu_{13}}$.
\item 11's and 13's order isomorphisms are both called $p$, and 11's $\rho$ and 13's
$\rho$ are different maps; the renamings above separate them.
\item 13 writes the factorization as $\sigma=U\circ M$, 12 and \cref{opa:thm:factorization}
as $\sigma=M\circ u$; the two agree with $U=MuM^{-1}$, since the monomial maps normalize
$\UU_\Oz$.
\item 11's target group $\Lambda$ is $\Delta$ here, 13's letter, so that $\lambda$ and
$\Lambda$ stay free; 11's Gaussian ring $J_\Gamma$ and field $L_\Gamma$ are
$\mathcal R^\C_\Gamma$ and $K^\C_\Gamma$.
\item The large-cardinal report's sign-sequence embedding, called $J$ there and in its
dossier, is written $\hat\jmath$ here, to keep $J$ for the strong embeddings of this part.
\end{itemize}

\section{Constant-term tests detect Hahn summability}\label{opa:as:sec:detect}

This section is 12's Section 3 and 13's Section 3, printed once. Let $k$ be any field and
$K=\Hahn k\Gamma$ a full Hahn field; $\Gamma$ may be a set or $\No$, with set-sized
supports. The point is that one scalar functional, $\ct$, together with multiplication,
recognizes Hahn summability.

\begin{lemma}[Finite intersection \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:lem:intersection}
If $S,T\subseteq\Gamma$ are well ordered, then $S\cap(-T)$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
An infinite subset of the well-ordered $S$ contains a strictly increasing sequence
$s_0<s_1<\cdots$, and then $-s_0>-s_1>\cdots$ would be a descending sequence in $T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Triangular detector \src{12}]\label{opa:as:lem:triangular}
Let $(x_j)_{j\in I}$ be a set-indexed family in $K$ in which each exponent occurs in only
finitely many supports. If $\bigcup_j\supp x_j$ is not well ordered, there is
$y\in K$ with countable support and all coefficients in $\{0,1\}$ such that
$\pairing{x_j}y\ne0$ for infinitely many $j$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose a strictly decreasing sequence in the union of the supports. Each support meets it
in finitely many places, since an infinite subsequence would descend inside a well order.
Select recursively a decreasing subsequence $g_0>g_1>\cdots$ and distinct indices
$j_0,j_1,\ldots$ with
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:triangle}
 \coef{g_n}{x_{j_n}}\ne0,\qquad \coef{g_m}{x_{j_n}}=0\quad(m>n):
\end{equation}
at each stage discard the finitely many places lying in the previously selected
supports, and pick a later place and an index whose support contains it. Choose
$c_n\in\{0,1\}$ recursively with $\sum_{m\le n}c_m\coef{g_m}{x_{j_n}}\ne0$; the new
variable $c_n$ has the nonzero coefficient $\coef{g_n}{x_{j_n}}$, so at most one of the
two choices is forbidden, over every field. Put $y=\sum_nc_nt^{-g_n}$, a Hahn series
because the exponents $-g_n$ increase. By \eqref{opa:as:eq:triangle},
$\pairing{x_{j_n}}y$ is exactly that finite sum, nonzero for every $n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Binary finite-row lemma \src{13}]\label{opa:as:lem:binary}
Let $(L_n)_{n\in\N}$ be nonzero linear forms on $k^J$, each using only finitely many
coordinates. There is $a\in\{0,1\}^J$ with $L_n(a)\ne0$ for infinitely many $n$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $E_n\ne\emptyset$ be the finite support of $L_n$. If $E=\bigcup_nE_n$ is finite, some
coordinate $j$ has a nonzero coefficient in infinitely many rows, and $a=e_j$ works. If
$E$ is infinite, select rows $n_0<n_1<\cdots$ inductively. At stage $r$ finitely many
coordinates have been assigned, and the rows after $n_{r-1}$ still use infinitely many
coordinates; choose $n_r$ whose support contains an unassigned coordinate $j_r$, set the
other new coordinates of that row to $0$, and choose $a_{j_r}\in\{0,1\}$ so that
$L_{n_r}(a)\ne0$ (the two choices change $L_{n_r}(a)$ by a nonzero field element). Mark all
of $E_{n_r}$ as assigned; later choices do not change this row. Finally set the remaining
coordinates to $0$.
\end{proof}

The lemma holds in every characteristic, including over $\mathbb F_2$, and uses no
uncountability, genericity or analytic argument. Asking for \emph{every} row to be nonzero
would be false: over $\mathbb F_2$ the forms $x$, $y$, $x+y$ cannot all be nonzero
\src{13}.

\begin{theorem}[Scalar detection of Hahn summability \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:thm:detect}
For a set-indexed family $(x_j)_{j\in I}$ in the full Hahn field $K$ the following are
equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},itemsep=1pt]
\item $(x_j)$ is Hahn summable;
\item for every $y\in K$, only finitely many $j$ have $\pairing{x_j}y\ne0$;
\item the same holds for every $y$ with countable support and coefficients in $\{0,1\}$.
\end{enumerate}
If they hold, then for every $y$
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:pairingsum}
 \Bigl\langle\sum_jx_j,y\Bigr\rangle_0=\sum_j\pairing{x_j}y,
\end{equation}
a finite scalar sum, and $\sum_jx_j$ is the unique element with this property.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
(i)$\Rightarrow$(ii): with $S=\bigcup_j\supp x_j$ and $T=\supp y$, only exponents in the
finite set $S\cap(-T)$ contribute to a constant term (\cref{opa:as:lem:intersection}), and
each occurs in finitely many $x_j$; all rearrangements in \eqref{opa:as:eq:pairingsum} are
finite. (ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii) is trivial. (iii)$\Rightarrow$(i): the tests $y=t^{-g}$
give pointwise finiteness, and if the union of the supports were not well ordered,
\cref{opa:as:lem:triangular} would give a countable binary $y$ violating (iii).
Uniqueness follows from the tests $t^{-g}$ and nondegeneracy.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second route to \textup{(iii)$\Rightarrow$(i)} \src{13}]
Assume (iii); monomial tests give pointwise finiteness. If the union $S$ is not well
ordered, choose $\gamma_0>\gamma_1>\cdots$ in $S$. Each support meets
$\{\gamma_n\}$ finitely, so infinitely many distinct indices $j_0,j_1,\ldots$ meet it. The
forms $L_r(a)=\sum_{n:\gamma_n\in\supp x_{j_r}}a_n\coef{\gamma_n}{x_{j_r}}$ on $k^\N$ are
nonzero and use finitely many coordinates, so \cref{opa:as:lem:binary} gives binary $a_n$
with infinitely many $L_r(a)\ne0$. For $y=\sum_na_nt^{-\gamma_n}$, a Hahn series, one has
$\ct(yx_{j_r})=L_r(a)$, contradicting (iii).
\end{proof}

The two routes differ in how cancellation is prevented: 12 first makes the family
triangular and then chooses coefficients; 13 keeps the family and chooses coefficients
row by row. 12's witness is also countable and binary, although 12 does not state it;
13 does. Every witness has countable set support, so the theorem holds for
$\Gamma=\No$ \src{12, 13}. A third route, 14's induced matching, whose witness has all
coefficients $1$ and involves no choice of coefficients, is printed in
\cref{opa:cm:sec:matching}.

\begin{corollary}[Countable certificates \src{13}]\label{opa:as:cor:countable}
Every nonsummable set-indexed family has a countable subfamily and a countably supported
binary $y$ for which infinitely many products $x_jy$ have nonzero constant term.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
An exponent occurring in infinitely many supports gives a monomial witness; otherwise use
\cref{opa:as:lem:triangular}.
\end{proof}

This does not say that summable families are countable: a Hahn sum may have an arbitrarily
large set-sized support. It says that a failure of summability has a countable
certificate.

\begin{example}[Monomial tests are not enough \src{13}]\label{opa:as:ex:monomialtests}
The family $x_n=t^{-n}$, $n\ge1$, has each exponent in one member, but its supports
descend. Each monomial test detects at most one member, whereas the Hahn series
$y=\sum_{n\ge1}t^n$ has $\ct(yx_n)=1$ for every $n$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Finiteness spaces \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:rem:finiteness}
\Cref{opa:as:lem:intersection} is finite-intersection orthogonality, and its linearization
is the finiteness-space duality of Ehrhard, applied to generalized power series by Blute,
Cockett, Jacqmin and Scott \cite{BCJS}. Neither support duality nor the notion of a strong
linear map is new; what the sources prove directly is the scalar criterion with its
cancellation argument and set supports over the class $\No$. It is an external
characterization of summation, not first-order definability of summation, and not
convergence in the valuation topology: a Hahn-summable family need not converge in that
topology \src{13}.
\end{remark}

\section{Constant-term-covariant isomorphisms are strong}\label{opa:as:sec:ctiso}

\begin{theorem}[Automatic strongness from constant-term covariance \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:thm:ctiso}
Let $K=\Hahn k\Gamma$ and $L=\Hahn l\Delta$ be full Hahn fields, the exponent groups sets or
$\No$, with set supports. Let $F:K\to L$ be a field isomorphism and $\alpha:k\to l$ a field
isomorphism with
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:ctcov}
 \ct(F x)=\alpha(\ct x)\qquad(x\in K).
\end{equation}
Then $F(r)=\alpha(r)$ for all $r\in k$, $F$ is $\alpha$-semilinear, and $F$ and $F^{-1}$ are
strongly additive.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $r\in k$ and $x\in K$, multiplicativity and \eqref{opa:as:eq:ctcov} give
$\ct\bigl((F(r)-\alpha(r))F(x)\bigr)=\alpha(\ct(rx))-\alpha(r)\alpha(\ct x)=0$. As $F$ is
onto, $F(r)-\alpha(r)$ pairs to zero with all of $L$, so $F(r)=\alpha(r)$; semilinearity
follows. Let $(x_j)$ be summable with sum $s$. For $y\in L$ write $y=F(z)$; then
$\pairing{F(x_j)}y=\alpha(\pairing{x_j}z)$ is nonzero for finitely many $j$
(\cref{opa:as:thm:detect} in $K$), so $(F(x_j))$ is summable
(\cref{opa:as:thm:detect} in $L$). With $w=\sum_jF(x_j)$,
$\pairing{F(s)}y=\alpha\bigl(\sum_j\pairing{x_j}z\bigr)=\sum_j\pairing{F(x_j)}y=\pairing wy$
for all $y$, so $F(s)=w$. The inverse satisfies the hypotheses with $\alpha^{-1}$.
\end{proof}

13 and 14 prove this with the extra hypothesis $F(k)=l$ and $\alpha=F|_k$; 12 derives it. The
proof detects summability of the image family before any target sum is used, and it
does not represent arbitrary strong functionals, which would fail for $\No$
(\cref{opa:as:thm:classdefect}). Surjectivity enters twice: it supplies a preimage of every
target test $y$, and it identifies the image of $k$. An embedding supplies only the tests
in its image; nondegeneracy of the pairing on the image does not detect all target
support obstructions. This is a real boundary, not a removable word: the embeddings of
\cref{opa:as:sec:explicit} satisfy \eqref{opa:as:eq:ctcov} with $\alpha=\id$ and move real
coefficients; they are strongly additive, so they contradict coefficient rigidity without
surjectivity, not strongness \src{12, 13}.

\begin{corollary}[The summation operation is recovered \src{12, 14}]\label{opa:as:cor:recover}
An isomorphism of full Hahn fields that carries the coefficient field onto the coefficient
field and commutes with the constant term up to that coefficient isomorphism respects the
whole set-indexed Hahn summation. It need not preserve the monomial sections.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proposition}[Coefficient matrix \src{13}]\label{opa:as:prop:matrix}
If in \cref{opa:as:thm:ctiso} $k=l$ and $\alpha=\id$, then for all $f\in K$ and
$\delta\in\Delta$
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:matrix}
 \coef\delta{F(f)}=\ct\bigl(F^{-1}(t^{-\delta})\,f\bigr)
 =\sum_{\gamma\in\supp f}\coef\gamma f\,\coef{-\gamma}{F^{-1}(t^{-\delta})},
 \qquad
 \coef\delta{F(t^\gamma)}=\coef{-\gamma}{F^{-1}(t^{-\delta})},
\end{equation}
with finite sums. So the coefficient matrix of $F$ is the inverse transpose of that of
$F^{-1}$, with exponent reversal from the pairing.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
$\coef\delta{F(f)}=\ct(F(f)t^{-\delta})=\ct\bigl(F(fF^{-1}(t^{-\delta}))\bigr)
=\ct\bigl(fF^{-1}(t^{-\delta})\bigr)$ by \eqref{opa:as:eq:ctcov}; expand the last constant
term, which has finitely many contributions by \cref{opa:as:lem:intersection}.
\end{proof}

The formula presupposes the global well-ordering of the image, which
\cref{opa:as:thm:ctiso} proves; a term-by-term formal argument alone would leave both the
coefficient and the well-ordering gaps open \src{13}.

\section{Automatic strongness of the omnific stabilizer}\label{opa:as:sec:auto}

\subsection{What the integer part reconstructs}

All three sources reprove the reconstruction of the coefficients from the integer part:
$\Pi=\bigcap_nn\Oz$ (\cref{opa:lem:divisible}), the existential test
$\exists y\,(x^2=2y^2)$ (\lbl{odg:def:thm:ideal}), the multiplier ring $(\Pi:\Pi)=\Ak$ with
units $\R^\times$ (\lbl{odg:def:thm:multiplier}), $\Frac\Oz=\No$ (\lbl{odg:thm:fractions};
also \cite[Proposition 2.4.5]{LM}, which records it as \cite[Theorem 32]{Conway}), and the
fixing of $\R$ by every automorphism (\lbl{odg:def:thm:autreal}, \cref{opa:cor:allautos}).
They credit these as prior work, and they are not reprinted. The one form needed below
that is not already stated in the report is 13's version for isomorphisms between
different, possibly non-divisible groups.

\begin{proposition}[Pair isomorphisms preserve the constant term \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:prop:setpairs}
Let $\Gamma,\Delta$ be nonzero ordered abelian groups, sets or $\No$, and let
$F:K_\Gamma\to K_\Delta$ be an ordered field isomorphism of the real Hahn fields with
$F(\mathcal R_\Gamma)=\mathcal R_\Delta$. Then $F$ fixes $\R$ pointwise, maps $\Pi_\Gamma$,
$\R$ and $\mm_\Gamma$ onto $\Pi_\Delta$, $\R$ and $\mm_\Delta$, and $\ct(Fx)=\ct(x)$ for all
$x$. If $\Gamma$ and $\Delta$ are divisible, ``ordered'' may be omitted.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{opa:lem:divisible}, $\Pi_\Gamma=\bigcap_nn\mathcal R_\Gamma$ for every ordered
abelian group, so $F(\Pi_\Gamma)=\Pi_\Delta$. The multiplier description
$\{x:x\Pi_\Gamma\subseteq\Pi_\Gamma\}=\mathcal A_\Gamma$ holds for every nonzero $\Gamma$: a
nonzero coefficient of $x$ at $\delta>0$ gives $\ct(xt^{-\delta})\ne0$. As $F$ is onto,
$F(\mathcal A_\Gamma)=\mathcal A_\Delta$, and the units $\R^\times$ correspond, so $F$ maps
$\R$ onto $\R$; a field isomorphism $\R\to\R$ is the identity. The order is preserved by
hypothesis, or because divisible groups give real closed Hahn fields whose order is
defined by squares. The infinitesimals $\{x:|x|<1/n\text{ for all }n\}$ are $\mm_\Gamma$,
so $F(\mm_\Gamma)=\mm_\Delta$, and the decomposition $K=\Pi\oplus\R\oplus\mm$ gives the
rest.
\end{proof}

For $\Gamma=\Delta$ divisible this is also a consequence of the parameter-free definitions
of \cref{opa:thm:projections}; for $\Gamma=\Delta=\No$ it is part of
\cref{opa:cor:allautos}. The coefficient field is reconstructed as a set and then fixed
pointwise; an ordered embedding $\R\hookrightarrow\No$ need not have image $\R$, and
\cref{opa:as:sec:explicit} gives embeddings that move reals by infinitesimals \src{13}.

\subsection{The main theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Automatic strongness of omnific automorphisms \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:thm:main}
Every unital ring automorphism $\sigma$ of $\Oz$ extends uniquely to a field automorphism
$\widetilde\sigma$ of $\No$. The extension preserves the order and fixes $\R$ pointwise,
and $\widetilde\sigma$ and $\widetilde\sigma^{-1}$ are strongly $\R$-linear: for every
Hahn-summable family $(f_j)$ the family $(\widetilde\sigma(f_j))$ is summable and
$\widetilde\sigma(\sum_jf_j)=\sum_j\widetilde\sigma(f_j)$. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:main}
 \Aut(\Oz)\cong\Aut(\No;\Oz)=\Aut^{\mathrm{str}}_\R(\No;\Oz),
\end{equation}
by restriction, with extension to fractions as inverse; here $\Aut(\No;\Oz)$ denotes the
field automorphisms of $\No$ preserving $\Oz$ setwise and the right-hand side those among
them that are strongly $\R$-linear with strong inverse.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Extension, uniqueness, order and fixing of $\R$ are \lbl{odg:def:thm:autreal}. By
\cref{opa:cor:allautos} (or \cref{opa:as:prop:setpairs}) the extension commutes with
$P_0=\ct$, without any strongness assumption. \Cref{opa:as:thm:ctiso} with
$k=l=\R$ and $\alpha=\id$ gives strongness of $\widetilde\sigma$ and its inverse.
\end{proof}

The dependencies are not circular \src{13, 14}:
\[
 \Oz\longrightarrow\Pi\longrightarrow\Ak\longrightarrow\R\longrightarrow
 (\Pi,\R,\mm)\longrightarrow\ct\longrightarrow\text{Hahn summation}.
\]
The first five steps use only algebra, the order and finite decompositions; the residue
criterion is used only after the constant term has been recovered. The group notation in
\eqref{opa:as:eq:main} is schematic, in the sense of \cref{opa:sub:foundations}: it
concerns individual class maps and their compositions, and forms no class of all of them.

\begin{remark}[What is and is not asserted \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:rem:main}
The theorem does not say that every automorphism of the field $\No$ is strong:
Kaplan, Krapp and Serra construct automorphisms of $\No$ that are not strongly additive
\cite[Example 4.4]{KKS}, and the theorem shows that none of them preserves $\Oz$. Nor does it
say that the automorphisms of $(\No,\Oz)$ are few: \cref{opa:part:aut} constructs large
families of them, and they are strong by construction. The theorem is a regularity
statement. It identifies the summation operation as preserved by these maps; invariance
under automorphisms is not first-order definability of summation. It also gives an
intrinsic certificate for a transported sum: a proposed value $g$ of
$\widetilde\sigma(\sum_jf_j)$ is correct exactly when
$\ct(yg)=\sum_j\ct(y\widetilde\sigma(f_j))$ for every $y\in\No$, each a finite equation
\src{13}. With \cref{opa:thm:criterion,opa:thm:factorization} the theorem answers
\cref{opa:q:strong}; see \cref{opa:as:sub:status}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Full real Hahn pairs \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:cor:setpairs}
Every isomorphism $F$ in \cref{opa:as:prop:setpairs} is strongly $\R$-linear with strongly
$\R$-linear inverse. In particular, for a nonzero divisible set-sized $\Gamma$, every field
automorphism of $K_\Gamma$ stabilizing $\mathcal R_\Gamma$ is strongly $\R$-linear.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
\Cref{opa:as:prop:setpairs} and \cref{opa:as:thm:ctiso} with $\alpha=\id$.
\end{proof}

The corollary assumes an isomorphism of the \emph{ambient} Hahn fields. It does not
assert that an abstract isomorphism $\mathcal R_\Gamma\cong\mathcal R_\Delta$, or an
automorphism of $\mathcal R_\Gamma$, extends to the Hahn fields when $\Frac\mathcal
R_\Gamma$ is smaller than $K_\Gamma$, which can happen (\cref{opa:as:ex:smallfrac},
\cite[Proposition 2.4.5]{LM}); 12 states the automorphism case, 13 the isomorphism case
\src{12, 13}.

\begin{corollary}[Determination by monomials \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:cor:monodet}
Two automorphisms of $\Oz$ whose extensions agree on every Conway monomial are equal. An
$\Oz$-preserving field automorphism of $\No$ fixing every monomial is the identity.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Both extensions fix $\R$ and commute with the normal-form sum of every element.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[Constant-term preservation does not give preservation of $\Oz$ \src{13}]\label{opa:as:ex:ctnotOz}
Choose a $\Q$-linear $q:\R\to\R$ with $q(1)=0$ and $q(\sqrt2)=1$, by extending
$\{1,\sqrt2\}$ to a Hamel basis, and put $\theta(\gamma)=q(\ct\gamma)$, the constant term
of the exponent's own normal form. Then $\theta$ is additive, $\theta(1)=0$ and
$\theta(\sqrt2)=1$, and $\Psi(t^\gamma)=t^\gamma\exp(\theta(\gamma)t)$ defines a strong
$\R$-linear $1$-automorphism of $\No$ with inverse given by $-\theta$: it is the flow
$T^1$ of \cref{opa:prop:iterate} with $\delta=1$, which needs only $\theta(1)=0$, not
$\theta(C_1)=0$. A term at exponent $0$ in $\Psi(t^\gamma)$ requires $\gamma=-n$ with
$n\in\N$, and $\theta(-n)=0$; so $\ct\circ\Psi=\ct$ on monomials and, by strongness,
everywhere. But
\[
 \Psi(t^{-\sqrt2})=t^{-\sqrt2}\exp(-t)
\]
has coefficient $\tfrac12$ at the positive exponent $2-\sqrt2$, so $\Psi(\Oz)\not\subseteq\Oz$.
Constant-term preservation suffices for automatic strongness of isomorphisms, but not for
preservation of $\Oz$. The merge notes the agreement with \cref{opa:thm:shear}: here
$\theta(C_1)\ne0$, since $\sqrt2\in C_1$.
\end{example}

\section{The whole stabilizer}\label{opa:as:sec:factor}

\begin{theorem}[Factorization of every pair automorphism \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:thm:factor}
Let $\Gamma=\No$, or a nonzero divisible set-sized ordered abelian group. Every field
automorphism $\sigma$ of $K_\Gamma=\Hahn\R\Gamma$ with $\sigma(\mathcal R_\Gamma)=\mathcal
R_\Gamma$ factors uniquely as
\[
 \sigma=M_{\chi,\tau}\circ u,\qquad u\in\UU_{\mathcal R_\Z}(\Gamma),
\]
where $\tau(\gamma)=v(\sigma(t^\gamma))$ is an order automorphism of $\Gamma$ and
$\chi(\gamma)=\lc(\sigma(t^\gamma))$ is a positive character; $u$ satisfies the equivalent
conditions of \cref{opa:thm:criterion}, in particular $a_{u,\delta}(C_\delta)=0$ for every
$\delta>0$. Conversely every such composition preserves $\mathcal R_\Gamma$. So the
stabilizer is $\{M_{\chi,\tau}\}\ltimes\UU_{\mathcal R_\Z}(\Gamma)$, and
$\Aut(\Oz)\cong\UU_\Oz\rtimes\{M_{\chi,\tau}\}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \cref{opa:as:thm:main} or \cref{opa:as:cor:setpairs}, $\sigma$ is strong and fixes $\R$.
It preserves the order, hence Archimedean equivalence, which is expressed with ordinary
integers; so it maps the valuation ring onto itself and is valued in the sense of
\cref{opa:sec:factorization}. Every character into $\R^\times$ of a divisible group is
positive. \Cref{opa:thm:factorization} now applies, with $k=\R$ and $\oo=\Z$.
\end{proof}

This is \cref{opa:thm:factorization} and the second sentence of \cref{opa:cor:allautos}
without the hypothesis of strongness. 12 proves the factorization for $\No$ and set-sized
groups, 13 for $\No$, in the order $\sigma=U\circ M_{\chi,\tau}$
(\cref{opa:as:sub:notation}); 14 proves it for both, in the order $M\circ u$, reading the
leading data from order and the fixed reals, so the existence of the factorization needs no
strongness, and the composition law it states is the one after
\eqref{opa:eq:monomialmap}. All three reprove the convex-support criterion: 12 by the least
forbidden shift, which is source 10's route (\cref{opa:sc:thm:supportcut}), and 13 and 14 by a
polynomial in the rational parameter $q$ with infinitely many zeros, which is 04's route
(\cref{opa:thm:criterion}); those proofs are printed there. 13's nontrivial example, the shear
$t^\gamma\mapsto t^\gamma\exp(q_1(\gamma)t)$ with $\omega^\omega\mapsto\omega^\omega\exp(-\omega^{-1})$,
is \cref{opa:ex:omegaomega}.

\begin{remark}[Admissibility is retained \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:rem:admissible}
\Cref{opa:as:thm:factor} classifies \emph{existing} automorphisms. An arbitrary array of
additive $a_\delta$ with $a_\delta(C_\delta)=0$ is not thereby shown to define one: the
monomial substitution must be summable on every well-ordered support, multiplicative and
invertible with the same admissibility. Local convex vanishing is not proved sufficient,
and no class-wide exponential--logarithm correspondence is used or established
(\cref{opa:q:characters}). The theorem removes a hypothesis on actual automorphisms; it
licenses no unchecked infinite substitution.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Archimedean groups \src{12, 14}]\label{opa:as:cor:arch}
For a nonzero divisible Archimedean set-sized $\Gamma$, every field automorphism of
$\Hahn\R\Gamma$ stabilizing $\mathcal R_\Gamma$ is a monomial map $M_{\chi,\tau}$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
In \cref{opa:as:thm:factor}, $u\in\UU_{\mathcal R_\Z}(\Gamma)=\{\id\}$ by \cref{opa:thm:rank}.
\end{proof}

\Cref{opa:thm:rank} gave this among strong maps; automatic strongness removes the
qualification. No claim is made that omnific-preserving automorphisms are trivial: the
monomial factor, and in higher rank the factor $\UU$, can be large \src{12}. For $\Gamma=\Q$
this is the form $t^q\mapsto c^qt^{\lambda q}$ after \cref{opa:thm:factorization}, and for
$\Gamma=\R$ the character may be discontinuous (\cref{opa:cm:rem:real}) \src{14}.

\begin{corollary}[What an automorphism of $\Oz$ does inside $\Pi$ \mergetag]\label{opa:as:cor:invisible}
Every ring automorphism $\sigma$ of $\Oz$ satisfies $\ct\circ\sigma=\ct$; its extension fixes $\R$ and is $M_{\chi,\tau}\circ
u$, where $\tau$ is an order automorphism of $\No$, $\chi$ a positive character and $u\in\UU_\Oz$.
Thus on $\Pi$ it acts first by $u$, then by the exponent automorphism and positive monomial rescaling $M_{\chi,\tau}$.
Here $u$ is a strong leading-term-fixing automorphism whose logarithmic character has, for
every $g\ne0$, support in $H_g$, the elements infinitesimal relative to $g$
(\cref{opa:thm:criterion}, condition (v)); every such composition occurs, and every monomial
image stays in the signed Archimedean block of its exponent up to the action of $\tau$
(\cref{opa:sc:prop:blocks}).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
\Cref{opa:as:thm:factor} with $\Gamma=\No$, and \cref{opa:thm:criterion,opa:sc:prop:blocks}
for $u$.
\end{proof}

With \cref{opa:thm:parameters,opa:thm:product,opa:thm:nonsolvable} this describes the
automorphisms of $\Oz$ inside $\Pi$ as fully as \cref{opa:part:aut} describes the strong
ones; the admissibility of logarithmic data remains \cref{opa:q:characters}. Source 10's
\cref{opa:sc:rem:nonstrong} is subsumed: its monomial condition now holds for every
automorphism because every automorphism is strong.

\section{Constant-term adjoints and a proper-class obstruction}\label{opa:as:sec:adjoint}

In this section $\Gamma,\Delta$ are \emph{sets}, $K=\Hahn k\Gamma$ and $L=\Hahn k\Delta$
share the coefficient field $k$, and linearity is over $k$, not over $K$. This is the
scalar $k$-linear dual of a full Hahn field; the three-duals report
(\fn{docs/surcomplex/three-duals-of-hahn-vector-spaces}, \lbl{duals:}) studies $K$-linear
maps on vector-valued Hahn spaces and topological duals, a different convention, and no
identification with its functionals is asserted \src{12, 13}. A $k$-linear functional
$\lambda:K\to k$ is \emph{strong} if it sends every summable family to a finitely supported
scalar family and commutes with the finite sum. $T$ denotes a $k$-linear map $K\to L$ in
this section only.

\begin{proposition}[Scalar representability at set size \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:prop:scalarrep}
Every strong $k$-linear functional $\lambda$ on $K$ has a unique representation
$\lambda(x)=\ct(ax)$ with $a\in K$; conversely every such functional is strong.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Put $a_g=\lambda(t^{-g})$, a function on the set $\Gamma$. If its support contained
$g_0>g_1>\cdots$, the monomials $t^{-g_n}$, with increasing exponents, would form a summable
family with all images nonzero; so $a=\sum a_gt^g\in K$. Strongness on the normal-form family
of $x$ gives $\lambda(x)=\sum_g\coef gx\,a_{-g}=\ct(ax)$, a finite sum; uniqueness is
nondegeneracy, and the converse is \eqref{opa:as:eq:pairingsum}. 13 argues on the set
$\{\gamma:\lambda(t^\gamma)\ne0\}$, which meets every well-ordered set finitely and is
therefore reverse well ordered, by dependent choice.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Adjoint characterization \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:thm:adjoint}
A $k$-linear map $T:K\to L$ is strongly $k$-linear if and only if it has a
\emph{constant-term adjoint} $T^\dagger:L\to K$,
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:adjoint}
 \pairing{Tx}y=\pairing x{T^\dagger y}\qquad(x\in K,\ y\in L).
\end{equation}
The adjoint is unique, $k$-linear and strong; $T^{\dagger\dagger}=T$, $(ST)^\dagger=T^\dagger
S^\dagger$ and $(T+T')^\dagger=T^\dagger+T'^\dagger$. Multiplication by a fixed series is
self-adjoint, and a coefficient-fixing, constant-term-preserving field isomorphism has
adjoint its inverse.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $T$ is strong, $x\mapsto\ct(T(x)y)$ is a strong functional, represented by a unique
$T^\dagger y$ (\cref{opa:as:prop:scalarrep}); uniqueness gives linearity. Conversely, given
$T^\dagger$ and a summable $(x_j)$, the scalars $\pairing{Tx_j}y=\pairing{x_j}{T^\dagger y}$
vanish for almost all $j$, so $(Tx_j)$ is summable by \cref{opa:as:thm:detect}, and
\eqref{opa:as:eq:pairingsum} on both sides of \eqref{opa:as:eq:adjoint} gives
$T(\sum x_j)=\sum Tx_j$. Symmetry of the pairing makes $T$ the adjoint of $T^\dagger$, so
$T^\dagger$ is strong. The identities are substitutions; for an isomorphism $F$ with
$\ct\circ F=\ct$, $\ct(F(x)y)=\ct(xF^{-1}(y))$.
\end{proof}

The direction ``adjoint implies strong'' uses only \cref{opa:as:thm:detect} and holds for
$\No$ as well; the converse genuinely needs set-sized groups
(\cref{opa:as:thm:classdefect}) \src{13}. The theorem can be read as a concrete transpose
statement for the linearized finiteness space; transpose duality in finiteness spaces is not
claimed new \src{12}.

\begin{theorem}[A canonical linear retraction \src{12}]\label{opa:as:thm:retraction}
Let $\phi:K\hookrightarrow L$ be a strong $k$-linear field embedding and $e_\phi=\phi^\dagger(1)$.
Then $e_\phi\ne0$ and $\phi^\dagger(\phi(a)y)=a\,\phi^\dagger(y)$ for $a\in K$, $y\in L$. The map
\[
 \ret_\phi(y)=e_\phi^{-1}\phi^\dagger(y)
\]
is a strong $K$-linear retraction ($L$ a $K$-space through $\phi$), with $\ret_\phi\circ\phi=\id_K$.
If $\ct\circ\phi=\ct$ then $e_\phi=1$ and $\ret_\phi=\phi^\dagger$. If $\phi$ is not onto,
$\ret_\phi$ is not multiplicative.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For $b\in K$, $\ct(b\,\phi^\dagger(\phi(a)y))=\ct(\phi(ba)y)=\ct(ba\,\phi^\dagger(y))$, which gives the
projection formula by nondegeneracy; $\ct(e_\phi)=\ct(\phi(1))=1$. With $y=1$,
$\phi^\dagger(\phi(a))=ae_\phi$. Multiplication by a fixed series is strong, so $\ret_\phi$ is
strong and $K$-linear. If $\ct\circ\phi=\ct$, then $\ct(xe_\phi)=\ct(x)$ for all $x$ and
$e_\phi=1$. A multiplicative unital retraction would be an injective field homomorphism with
$\ret_\phi(y-\phi(\ret_\phi y))=0$, forcing $y=\phi(\ret_\phi y)$ for all $y$.
\end{proof}

13 proves the case $\ct\circ\phi=\ct$ with $\phi$ coefficient-fixing (its residue splitting);
it adds that $\ker\phi^\dagger$ is the annihilator of $\phi(K)$ for the pairing, and that
$L=\phi(K)\oplus\ker\phi^\dagger$ as $\phi(K)$-spaces. More generally a proper field extension
admits no unital ring retraction fixing the subfield \src{13}.

\begin{corollary}[Tower law and complement \src{12}]\label{opa:as:cor:tower}
For composable constant-term-preserving strong $k$-linear field embeddings
$K\xrightarrow{\phi}L\xrightarrow{\psi}P$, $\ret_{\psi\phi}=\ret_\phi\circ\ret_\psi$. For one such
embedding $L=\phi(K)\oplus\ker\ret_\phi$ as $K$-spaces, and $\phi\circ\ret_\phi$ is a strong
projection onto $\phi(K)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Adjoints reverse composition and all normalizing elements are $1$; the complement follows from
$\ret_\phi\circ\phi=\id$.
\end{proof}

$\ret_\phi$ is not a field retraction and not a positive conditional expectation; neither
multiplicativity nor positivity is part of its conclusion, and it is canonical only relative to
the two pairings and to $\phi$ \src{12}.

\begin{example}[Monomial embeddings \src{12, 13}]\label{opa:as:ex:monomialadjoint}
For an ordered group embedding $\tau:\Gamma\hookrightarrow\Delta$ and a character
$\chi:\Gamma\to k^\times$, the strong embedding $M_{\chi,\tau}$ preserves constant terms and
\[
 M_{\chi,\tau}^\dagger\Bigl(\sum_\eta y_\eta t^\eta\Bigr)=\sum_{g\in\Gamma}\chi(g)^{-1}y_{\tau(g)}t^g,
\]
supported in the preimage under an order embedding of a well-ordered set; compare constant
coefficients, using $\chi(-g)=\chi(g)^{-1}$. The same formula works for class groups with set
supports, by replacement on the target support \src{13}. If $\eta\notin\tau(\Gamma)$, then
$M_{\chi,\tau}^\dagger(t^\eta)=M_{\chi,\tau}^\dagger(t^{-\eta})=0$ while $M_{\chi,\tau}^\dagger(1)=1$, so
the retraction is not multiplicative on two monomials. For instance $\Gamma=\Delta=\Z$,
$\tau(g)=2g$, $\chi(g)=2^g$ over $k=\Q$ give a weighted even-coefficient extraction \src{12}.
\end{example}

For a constant-term-compatible embedding $\phi$ the source tests show only that
$\ct(\phi(h)\phi(x_j))$ is finitely supported in $j$ for each $h\in K$; detection in $L$ needs
all $y\in L$. An adjoint supplies a source representative of every target test, so the
question is whether all target residue functionals can be pulled back; this locates the
remaining embedding questions without answering them \src{13}
(\cref{opa:as:q:embeddingstrong,opa:as:q:targettests}).

\begin{theorem}[A strong functional with no representing surreal \src{12, 13, 14}]\label{opa:as:thm:classdefect}
For $x\in\No$ put
\begin{equation}\label{opa:as:eq:classfunctional}
 \lambda_{\On}(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\On}\coef{-\alpha}x .
\end{equation}
For each $x$ only finitely many terms are nonzero. $\lambda_{\On}$ is a strongly $\R$-linear
functional, but no $a\in\No$ satisfies $\lambda_{\On}(x)=\ct(ax)$ for all $x$. Hence the strong
$\R$-linear map $x\mapsto\lambda_{\On}(x)\cdot1$ of $\No$ has no constant-term adjoint.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A well-ordered set of exponents meets $-\On$ finitely, since an infinite intersection would give
a strictly decreasing sequence of ordinals. The same applies to the total support of a summable
family, and only finitely many members contribute at each of those exponents; this gives
strongness. As $\lambda_{\On}(t^{-\alpha})=1$ for every ordinal $\alpha$, a representing $a$ would
have $\coef\alpha a=1$ for all $\alpha$, a proper-class support. An adjoint evaluated at $1$ would
be such an $a$.
\end{proof}

The definition is not a proper-class Hahn sum: for each input one first finds the finite subset of
its support lying in $-\On$ and adds finitely many reals; the class only selects coefficients
\src{12}. There is no conflict with \cref{opa:as:thm:ctiso}: there the inverse of an isomorphism
supplies each needed test element, and no arbitrary strong functional is represented. So the
isomorphism theorem extends to $\No$ while the self-duality of
\cref{opa:as:prop:scalarrep,opa:as:thm:adjoint} does not \src{12, 13}.

\section{The bottom-gap classification of exact-monomial embeddings}\label{opa:as:sec:gap}

This section and the next three are source 11's, with the embeddings of 12 and 13 as
instances. Automorphisms preserving $\Oz$ fix $\R$ because $\R$ is parameter-free
definable in the pair (\lbl{odg:def:cor:internal}, \cref{opa:thm:projections}); the definition
uses a universally quantified multiplier condition, which a proper embedding need not preserve
\src{11, 12}, as the Diophantine report already cautions after \lbl{odg:def:thm:autreal}.
The existential part survives: by \lbl{odg:def:thm:ideal} and \lbl{odg:def:thm:homct}, every
unital ring homomorphism $\mathcal R_\Gamma\to\mathcal R_\Delta$ maps $\Pi_\Gamma$ into
$\Pi_\Delta$ and preserves the constant term \src{11}. Throughout, $\Gamma$ and $\Delta$ are
nonzero divisible ordered abelian groups (sets, or $\No$ with set supports), so $K_\Gamma$ and
$K_\Delta$ are real closed, and $\tau:\Gamma\hookrightarrow\Delta$ is an ordered additive
embedding.

\begin{definition}[Bottom gap \src{11}]\label{opa:as:def:gap}
The \emph{bottom gap} of $\tau$ is
\[
 \gap_\tau=\{c\in\Delta:n|c|<h\text{ for every }h\in\tau(\Gamma)^{>0}\text{ and every }n\ge1\}.
\]
Since $\tau(\Gamma)$ is divisible, this is $\{c:|c|<h\text{ for every }h\in\tau(\Gamma)^{>0}\}$.
\end{definition}

The word ``gap'' refers to a convex subgroup of the value group, not to an omitted cut of the
field \src{11}.

\begin{lemma}[Intrinsic description \src{11}]\label{opa:as:lem:gap}
$\gap_\tau$ is the largest convex subgroup of $\Delta$ meeting $\tau(\Gamma)$ only in $0$; it is
